7/18/24, 4:25 PM - 18mcmc27 Abhinav Gautam: To sort the list of students by name and age, you can use the `sorted()` method of the Stream API, like this:
```
students.stream()
        .sorted((s1, s2) -> {
            int nameCompare = s1.getName().compareTo(s2.getName());
            if (nameCompare == 0) {
                return Integer.compare(s1.getAge(), s2.getAge());
            } else {
                return nameCompare;
            }
        })
        .forEach(System.out::println);
```
This code sorts the list of students first by name, and then by age (in case of a tie on names).

Here's a breakdown of the code:

- `sorted()` method takes a comparator as an argument.
- The comparator compares two students (`s1` and `s2`).
- First, it compares their names using `compareTo()` method.
- If the names are equal (i.e., `nameCompare == 0`), it compares their ages using `Integer.compare()`.
- Finally, it returns the result of the comparison.

By using this comparator, the list of students will be sorted first by name, and then by age.

Note: This code assumes that the `Student` class has a `getName()` method that returns the student's name, and a `getAge()` method that returns the student's age.

7/18/24, 4:35 PM - 18mcmc27 Abhinav Gautam: Yes, there is another way to get only the students with unique names using a single stream pipeline:
```
students.stream()
        .collect(Collectors.toMap(Student::getName, Function.identity(), (a, b) -> a))
        .values()
        .forEach(System.out::println);
```
This code uses the `collect()` method to create a map where the keys are the student names and the values are the students themselves. The `(a, b) -> a` part is a merge function that keeps the first student with a given name (ignoring duplicates). Finally, the `values()` method is used to get a collection of the unique students, which are then printed.

This approach is more concise and efficient than the previous one, as it avoids the need for a nested stream.

7/18/24, 4:36 PM - 18mcmc27 Abhinav Gautam: In Java 7 and earlier, you can achieve the same result using a HashMap to keep track of unique names and a ArrayList to store the unique students:
```
Map<String, Student> uniqueStudents = new HashMap<String, Student>();
List<Student> uniqueStudentList = new ArrayList<Student>();

for (Student student : students) {
    if (!uniqueStudents.containsKey(student.getName())) {
        uniqueStudents.put(student.getName(), student);
        uniqueStudentList.add(student);
    }
}

for (Student student : uniqueStudentList) {
    System.out.println(student);
}
```
This code iterates over the list of students, checks if a student with the same name already exists in the `uniqueStudents` map, and if not, adds the student to both the map and the `uniqueStudentList`. Finally, it prints the unique students.

Note that this code uses a traditional for loop and explicit map and list operations, unlike the Java 8 stream-based solution.
7/18/24, 4:46 PM - 18mcmc27 Abhinav Gautam: Primary Key (PK) and Foreign Key (FK) are two types of keys in a relational database management system (RDBMS):

Primary Key (PK):

- A unique identifier for a record in a table
- Ensures data integrity by preventing duplicate records
- Must be unique, not null, and immutable
- Can be a single column or a combination of columns (composite key)
- Defines the identity of a record

Example: Employee ID (unique identifier for each employee)

Foreign Key (FK):

- A field in a table that references the primary key of another table
- Establishes a relationship between two tables
- Ensures data consistency by linking related records
- Can be null or duplicate (depending on the relationship)
- Defines the relationship between records in different tables

Example: Department ID (in the Employee table) references the Department ID (in the Department table)

Key differences:

- PK defines the identity of a record, while FK defines the relationship between records
- PK is unique, while FK can be duplicate or null
- PK is in the same table, while FK references a column in another table

In summary, the primary key uniquely identifies a record, while the foreign key links related records across tables, maintaining data integrity and consistency.

7/20/24, 5:06 PM - Vimlendra Dubey Hcu Mca: The choice between using a linked list for traversing and an array for insertion depends on the specific requirements and advantages of each data structure. Here's an explanation of why one might use a linked list for traversing and an array for insertion:

### Linked List for Traversing

1. **Dynamic Size**: Linked lists can grow or shrink in size dynamically, making it easy to traverse without worrying about the capacity.
2. **Efficient Insertion/Deletion**: Inserting or deleting nodes during traversal (except for insertion at the end) is efficient and involves changing the pointers, which is O(1) time complexity.
3. **Memory Usage**: Linked lists use memory proportionally to the number of elements, without the need for contiguous memory blocks.

### Array for Insertion

1. **Random Access**: Arrays allow direct access to elements using indices, which makes it faster to insert elements at specific positions if the position is known.
2. **Cache Performance**: Arrays are stored in contiguous memory locations, which leads to better cache performance.
3. **Simpler Implementation**: Array-based insertion can be simpler to implement in scenarios where the maximum size is known and memory reallocation is not a concern.

### Why Not Use Linked List for Insertion?

- **Random Access Limitation**: Linked lists do not support efficient random access. Accessing an element at a specific index requires O(n) time, which makes inserting elements at arbitrary positions inefficient.
- **Overhead of Pointers**: Each node in a linked list requires additional memory for the pointer, which can lead to higher memory consumption compared to arrays, especially for small data elements.

### Why Not Use Array for Traversing?

- **Fixed Size**: Arrays have a fixed size, and resizing them requires creating a new array and copying the elements, which is inefficient.
- **Insertion/Deletion Overhead**: Inserting or deleting elements (especially not at the end) requires shifting elements, leading to O(n) time complexity.

### Combining Both

In some applications, a hybrid

7/22/24, 11:21 AM - 18mcmc11 Abhishek Pathak: Scrum ceremonies, also known as Scrum events, are structured meetings designed to facilitate the Scrum process. Here are the main Scrum ceremonies and their purposes:

1. **Sprint Planning**:
   - **Purpose**: To plan the work to be performed during the sprint.
   - **Attendees**: Product Owner, Scrum Master, Development Team.
   - **Key Activities**:
     - The Product Owner presents the highest priority items from the product backlog.
     - The team discusses and estimates the effort required for each item.
     - The team commits to a set of items to complete in the sprint.

2. **Daily Scrum (Stand-up Meeting)**:
   - **Purpose**: To synchronize the team's activities and plan for the next 24 hours.
   - **Attendees**: Development Team, Scrum Master (optional), Product Owner (optional).
   - **Key Activities**:
     - Each team member answers three questions: What did I do yesterday? What will I do today? Are there any impediments in my way?

3. **Sprint Review**:
   - **Purpose**: To inspect the work completed during the sprint and gather feedback.
   - **Attendees**: Product Owner, Scrum Master, Development Team, Stakeholders.
   - **Key Activities**:
     - The team demonstrates the completed work.
     - Stakeholders provide feedback.
     - The Product Owner discusses the current state of the product backlog.

4. **Sprint Retrospective**:
   - **Purpose**: To reflect on the sprint and identify ways to improve.
   - **Attendees**: Development Team, Scrum Master, Product Owner (optional).
   - **Key Activities**:
     - The team discusses what went well, what didn’t go well, and what could be improved.
     - The team identifies actionable improvement items for the next sprint.

5. **Backlog Refinement (Grooming)**:
   - **Purpose**: To keep the product backlog updated and prepare items for future sprints.
   - **Attendees**: Product Owner, Scrum Master, Development Team.
   - **Key Activities**:
     - The team reviews and clarifies backlog items.
     - Items are broken down into smaller tasks, estimated, and prioritized.

These ceremonies help ensure transparency, inspection, and adaptation, which are core principles of Scrum. They promote collaboration and continuous improvement within the team.
7/22/24, 11:21 AM - 18mcmc27 Abhinav Gautam: The ceremonies in Scrum are:

1. *Sprint Planning*: The team plans the work for the upcoming Sprint, sets Sprint Goals, and commits to delivering a specific set of work.

2. *Daily Scrum*: A daily meeting where team members share their progress, plans, and any obstacles they're facing.

3. *Sprint Review*: A meeting where the team showcases the work completed during the Sprint and receives feedback from stakeholders.

4. *Sprint Retrospective*: A meeting where the team reflects on the Sprint, identifies improvements, and implements changes to enhance their processes.

These ceremonies provide a framework for the Scrum team to collaborate, inspect, and adapt their work, ensuring continuous improvement and delivery of value to stakeholders.

Note: These ceremonies are time-boxed, meaning they have a maximum duration, to ensure the team stays focused and efficient.

7/22/24, 11:28 AM - 18mcmc27 Abhinav Gautam: The steps of Continuous Integration (CI) are:

1. *Source*: Developers commit code changes to a version control system (e.g., Git).

2. *Build*: The CI server (e.g., Jenkins, Travis CI) automatically builds the project from the source code.

3. *Test*: Automated tests (unit tests, integration tests, etc.) are run to ensure the code changes didn't break the build.

4. *Package*: The built software is packaged into a deployable format (e.g., JAR, WAR, Docker image).

5. *Deploy*: The packaged software is deployed to a staging or production environment.

6. *Verify*: Automated verification steps (e.g., smoke tests, acceptance tests) ensure the deployed software functions correctly.

7. *Report*: The CI server generates reports on the build, test, and deployment results.

8. *Repeat*: The CI process starts again with the next code commit.

These steps help ensure that code changes are thoroughly tested, validated, and deployed quickly and reliably, improving software quality and development efficiency.

1. *Long Method*: Methods that are too long and complex, indicating a need for refactoring.
2. *Duplicate Code*: Identical code in multiple places, making maintenance harder.
3. *Switch Statements with Many Cases*: Long switch statements with many cases, indicating a need for polymorphism.
4. *God Object*: Classes that have too many responsibilities and contain a large amount of code.
5. *Shotgun Surgery*: Multiple small changes in multiple places to fix a problem, indicating a need for a more comprehensive solution.
6. *Feature Envy*: Methods that use or modify data from other classes excessively, indicating a need for better encapsulation.
7. *Golden Hammer*: Using a single solution or approach for all problems, even when it's not the best fit.
8. *Magic Number*: Using unexplained numerical values in code, making it hard to understand and maintain.
9. *Long Parameter List*: Methods with too many parameters, indicating a need for simplification.
10. *Dead Code*: Unused code that is not executed, making the codebase harder to understand.
11. *Speculative Generality*: Adding code for hypothetical future needs, making the codebase more complex.
12. *Over-Engineering*: Overly complex solutions for simple problems.

By recognizing and addressing code smells, developers can improve the maintainability, readability, and overall quality of their codebase.

```
import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;

public class MyTest {

    @Test
    public void testAddition() {
        int result = 2 + 2;
        assertEquals(4, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void testStringEquality() {
        String expected = "Hello";
        String actual = "Hello";
        assertEquals(expected, actual);
    }

    @Test
    public void testArrayEquality() {
        int[] expected = {1, 2, 3};
        int[] actual = {1, 2, 3};
        assertArrayEquals(expected, actual);
    }
}
```

- `testStringEquality`: Tests that two strings are equal
- `testArrayEquality`: Tests that two arrays are equal

The `assertEquals` method is used to check that the expected result matches the actual result. The `assertArrayEquals` method is used to check that two arrays are equal.

```
import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertThrows;

public class CalculatorTest {

    @Test
    public void testDivideByZero() {
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        assertThrows(CalculationException.class, () -> {
            calculator.divide(10, 0);
        });
    }
}
```

In this example:

- We create a `Calculator` object.
- We use the `assertThrows` method to verify that a `CalculationException` is thrown when we call the `divide` method with a divisor of zero.
- The lambda expression `() -> { calculator.divide(10, 0); }` represents the code that we expect to throw the exception.

By using `assertThrows`, we can ensure that our `Calculator` class correctly handles division by zero and throws the expected exception.

Note: `CalculationException` is a custom exception that you would need to define separately.
7/22/24, 11:41 AM - 18mcmc27 Abhinav Gautam: [22/07, 11:41 am] Meta AI: If a method has a return type of `void`, it means that the method doesn't return any value. In such cases, you can't use `assertEquals` to verify the return value. Instead, you can test the method's behavior by:

1. Verifying the side effects: Check if the method has changed the state of the object or has performed the expected actions.
2. Using a mock object: Mock the dependencies of the method and verify that the method has interacted with them as expected.
3. Checking the output: If the method writes to a file, network, or console, verify that the output is as expected.

Here's an example:

```
import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.mockito.Mockito.verify;

public class MyServiceTest {

    @Test
    public void testDoSomething() {
        MyService myService = new MyService();
        MyDependency dependency = mock(MyDependency.class);
        myService.setDependency(dependency);
        
        myService.doSomething();
        
        verify(dependency).performAction();
    }
}
```

In this example:

- We create a `MyService` object and a mock object for its dependency.
- We call the `doSomething` method, which has a return type of `void`.
- We use Mockito's `verify` method to check that the `dependency` object's `performAction` method was called as expected.

By using mocking and verification, we can ensure that our `MyService` class correctly interacts with its dependencies even if the method returns `void`.

7/22/24, 11:45 AM - 18mcmc11 Abhishek Pathak: In Hibernate, many-to-many relationships are mapped using a join table. Here’s how it’s typically done:

1. **Define Entities**: Create two entities with a many-to-many relationship.

2. **Create a Join Table**: Hibernate uses a join table to manage the relationship between the two entities.

**Example Entities**:

```java
import javax.persistence.*;
import java.util.Set;

@Entity
public class Student {
    @Id
    @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)
    private Long id;
    
    private String name;
    
    @ManyToMany
    @JoinTable(
        name = "student_course",
        joinColumns = @JoinColumn(name = "student_id"),
        inverseJoinColumns = @JoinColumn(name = "course_id")
    )
    private Set<Course> courses;

    // Getters and Setters
}

@Entity
public class Course {
    @Id
    @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)
    private Long id;
    
    private String title;
    
    @ManyToMany(mappedBy = "courses")
    private Set<Student> students;

    // Getters and Setters
}
```

**Explanation**:

- **`@ManyToMany`**: Indicates the many-to-many relationship.
- **`@JoinTable`**: Specifies the join table (`student_course`), along with the join columns (`student_id` and `course_id`).
- **`mappedBy`**: In the `Course` entity, it specifies that the `students` field is the inverse side of the relationship.

In this setup:
- The `Student` entity is associated with multiple `Course` entities.
- The `Course` entity is associated with multiple `Student` entities.
- The join table `student_course` manages the many-to-many relationship.
7/22/24, 11:46 AM - 18mcmc27 Abhinav Gautam: Here's an example of creating a thread using a lambda expression in Java:

```
Thread thread = new Thread(() -> {
    // Code to be executed in the thread
    System.out.println("Hello from thread!");
});

thread.start();
```

In this example, the lambda expression `() -> { ... }` is used to define the code that will be executed in the thread. The `Thread` constructor takes a `Runnable` object as an argument, and the lambda expression is converted to a `Runnable` automatically.

This is equivalent to creating a thread with an anonymous inner class:

```
Thread thread = new Thread(new Runnable() {
    public void run() {
        // Code to be executed in the thread
        System.out.println("Hello from thread!");
    }
});

thread.start();
```

Using lambda expressions can make the code more concise and easier to read.

1. **Default Logging Framework**: Spring Boot uses `Spring Boot Starter Logging`, which includes `Logback` as the default logging framework.

     # Set log level for specific packages
     logging.level.org.springframework.web=DEBUG
     logging.level.com.example=INFO

     # Set log file location
     logging.file.name=app.log
     ```

   - **`logback-spring.xml`**: For more advanced configurations, you can use a custom Logback configuration file.
     ```xml
     <configuration>
         <appender name="FILE" class="ch.qos.logback.core.FileAppender">

7/22/24, 11:55 AM - 18mcmc11 Abhishek Pathak: To inject values from `application.yaml` into your Spring Boot application classes, follow these steps:

1. **Define Properties in `application.yaml`**:
   ```yaml
   myapp:
     server:
       port: 8080
     database:
       url: jdbc:mysql://localhost:3306/mydb
       username: user
       password: pass
   ```

2. **Create a Configuration Properties Class**:
   Use the `@ConfigurationProperties` annotation to bind these properties to a Java class. This class will hold the configuration values.

   ```java
   import org.springframework.boot.context.properties.ConfigurationProperties;
   import org.springframework.stereotype.Component;

   @Component
   @ConfigurationProperties(prefix = "myapp")
   public class MyAppProperties {
       private Server server;
       private Database database;

       // Getters and setters

       public static class Server {
           private int port;

           // Getter and setter
           public int getPort() {
               return port;
           }
           public void setPort(int port) {
               this.port = port;
           }
       }

       public static class Database {
           private String url;
           private String username;
           private String password;

           // Getters and setters
           public String getUrl() {
               return url;
           }
           public void setUrl(String url) {
               this.url = url;
           }
           public String getUsername() {
               return username;
           }
           public void setUsername(String username) {
               this.username = username;
           }
           public String getPassword() {
               return password;
           }
           public void setPassword(String password) {
               this.password = password;
           }
       }

       public Server getServer() {
           return server;
       }

       public void setServer(Server server) {
           this.server = server;
       }

       public Database getDatabase() {
           return database;
       }

       public void setDatabase(Database database) {
           this.database = database;
       }
   }
   ```

3. **Enable Configuration Properties**:
   Add `@EnableConfigurationProperties` to your main application class or a configuration class to enable support for `@ConfigurationProperties`.

   ```java
   import org.springframework.boot.SpringApplication;
   import org.springframework.boot.autoconfigure.SpringBootApplication;
   import org.springframework.boot.context.properties.EnableConfigurationProperties;

   @SpringBootApplication
   @EnableConfigurationProperties(MyAppProperties.class)
   public class MyApplication {
       public static void main(String[] args) {
           SpringApplication.run(MyApplication.class, args);
       }
   }
   ```

4. **Inject the Configuration Properties into Your Components**:
   Use `@Autowired` to inject the properties class into your Spring-managed beans.

   ```java
   import org.springframework.beans.factory.annotation.Autowired;
   import org.springframework.stereotype.Service;

   @Service
   public class MyService {

       private final MyAppProperties properties;

       @Autowired
       public MyService(MyAppProperties properties) {
           this.properties = properties;
       }

       public void printProperties() {
           System.out.println("Server Port: " + properties.getServer().getPort());
           System.out.println("Database URL: " + properties.getDatabase().getUrl());
       }
   }
   ```

In this setup:
- **`@ConfigurationProperties`** maps YAML properties to a Java object.
- **`@Component`** makes the properties class a Spring-managed bean.
- **`@EnableConfigurationProperties`** ensures the properties class is recognized by Spring.
- **`@Autowired`** injects the properties class into other beans where needed.

7/22/24, 12:10 PM - 18mcmc11 Abhishek Pathak: Organizations migrate from monolithic to microservices architectures for several reasons:

1. **Scalability**: Microservices allow scaling of individual components independently, making it easier to handle varying loads.

2. **Flexibility**: Teams can develop, deploy, and scale services independently, enabling faster updates and innovations.

3. **Resilience**: Isolated services mean that failures in one service do not necessarily bring down the entire system, improving overall system resilience.

4. **Technology Diversity**: Microservices allow the use of different technologies and languages for different services, optimizing for specific requirements.

5. **Development Speed**: Smaller, focused teams can work on different services simultaneously, speeding up development and deployment cycles.

6. **Deployment
7/22/24, 12:11 PM - 18mcmc11 Abhishek Pathak: Disadvantage of microservices
7/22/24, 12:11 PM - 18mcmc46 Manish Choumiyan: Here are some disadvantages of using microservices:

1. **Complexity**: Managing multiple services can be complex, especially when they interact frequently.
2. **Deployment Challenges**: Coordinating deployments of numerous services can be difficult.
3. **Data Consistency**: Ensuring data consistency across services can be tricky, often requiring complex transactions or eventual consistency.
4. **Increased Overhead**: Each service requires its own resources, leading to higher memory and CPU usage.
5. **Networking Issues**: Increased network communication can lead to latency and reliability issues.
6. **Debugging and Testing**: Troubleshooting and testing across distributed services can be more challenging compared to a monolithic architecture.

7/22/24, 12:12 PM - 18mcmc46 Manish Choumiyan: Here are some common load balancing techniques:

1. **Round Robin**: Distributes requests sequentially to each server in a rotating fashion.
2. **Least Connections**: Directs traffic to the server with the fewest active connections.
3. **IP Hash**: Uses the client's IP address to determine which server will handle the request, ensuring consistent routing for specific clients.

1. **Local Caching**: Store cached data within each service's local memory using libraries like Guava or Caffeine.

2. **Distributed Caching**: Use a shared cache that multiple services can access, such as Redis or Memcached. This ensures consistency and reduces duplication.

3. **Cache Aside Pattern**: Services check the cache before querying the database. If the data is not in the cache, they load it from the database and update the cache.

4. **Write Through/Write Behind Patterns**: Automatically update the cache when writing data to the database (write-through) or update the cache asynchronously (write-behind).

5. **TTL (Time-to-Live)**: Set expiration times for cached entries to ensure stale data is not served.

6. **Cache Invalidation**: Implement strategies to invalidate or refresh cache entries when underlying data changes to maintain data consistency.

7/22/24, 12:26 PM - 18mcmc46 Manish Choumiyan: Serialization in Java is the process of converting an object into a byte stream to be saved to a file, sent over a network, or otherwise persisted. The object can later be deserialized, which is the process of converting the byte stream back into a copy of the original object. 

To enable serialization:

1. **Implement `Serializable` Interface**: A class must implement `java.io.Serializable` to indicate that its instances can be serialized.
   ```java
   import java.io.Serializable;

   public class MyClass implements Serializable {
       private static final long serialVersionUID = 1L; // Optional but recommended
       private String name;
       private transient int age; // `transient` fields are not serialized
       
       // Getters and setters
   }
   ```

2. **Use `ObjectOutputStream` and `ObjectInputStream`**: These classes handle the actual serialization and deserialization process.
   ```java
   import java.io.*;

   public class SerializationExample {
       public static void main(String[] args) {
           MyClass obj = new MyClass();
           obj.setName("John Doe");
           obj.setAge(30);

           // Serialize
           try (ObjectOutputStream oos = new ObjectOutputStream(new FileOutputStream("object.ser"))) {
               oos.writeObject(obj);
           } catch (IOException e) {
               e.printStackTrace();
           }

           // Deserialize
           try (ObjectInputStream ois = new ObjectInputStream(new FileInputStream("object.ser"))) {
               MyClass deserializedObj = (MyClass) ois.readObject();
               System.out.println(deserializedObj.getName());
           } catch (IOException | ClassNotFoundException e) {
               e.printStackTrace();
           }
       }
   }
   ```

**Key Points**:
- **`serialVersionUID`**: This is a unique identifier for the serialized class, used for version control.
- **`transient` Keyword**: Fields marked as `transient` are not serialized.
- **Custom Serialization**: You can customize serialization by implementing `writeObject` and `readObject` methods.

7/22/24, 12:28 PM - 18mcmc46 Manish Choumiyan: **HashMap** and **Hashtable** differ in several ways:

- **Synchronization**: `HashMap` is not synchronized, making it faster but not thread-safe. `Hashtable` is synchronized, so it is thread-safe but slower due to synchronization overhead.

- **Null Keys/Values**: `HashMap` allows one null key and multiple null values. `Hashtable` does not allow null keys or values.

- **Performance**: `HashMap` generally offers better performance compared to `Hashtable` because it lacks synchronization.

- **Iterator**: `HashMap` uses a fail-fast iterator that throws `ConcurrentModificationException` if modified during iteration. `Hashtable` uses a non-fail-fast iterator.

- **Legacy Status**: `HashMap` is part of the Java Collections Framework introduced in Java 2, while `Hashtable` is a legacy class from earlier Java versions.
7/22/24, 12:28 PM - 18mcmc11 Abhishek Pathak: Here's a comparison of `HashMap` and `Hashtable` in Java presented in a table format:

| Feature              | `HashMap`                                | `Hashtable`                            |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------------|----------------------------------------|
| **Null Keys/Values** | Allows one `null` key and multiple `null` values. | Does not allow `null` keys or values.  |
| **Thread Safety**    | Not synchronized; not thread-safe.      | Synchronized; thread-safe by default.   |
| **Performance**      | Generally faster due to lack of synchronization. | Generally slower due to synchronization. |
| **Iterator**         | Iterator is fail-fast; throws `ConcurrentModificationException` if the map is modified during iteration. | Enumerator is not fail-fast. |
| **Legacy Status**    | Introduced in Java 1.2 as part of the Java Collections Framework. | Introduced in Java 1.0; part of the original version of Java. |
| **Default Capacity** | Default capacity is 16, with a load factor of 0.75. | Default capacity is 11, with a load factor of 0.75. |
| **Iteration**        | `forEach` method introduced in Java 8 for lambda support. | Does not have lambda support; uses old-style enumeration. |

**Summary**: Use `HashMap` for better performance and when you don't need thread safety. Use `Hashtable` if you need thread safety but are okay with potentially slower performance.

- **Synchronization**:
  - **StringBuilder**: Not synchronized, making it faster but not thread-safe.
  - **StringBuffer**: Synchronized, making it thread-safe but slower due to synchronization overhead.

- **Performance**:
  - **StringBuilder**: Typically performs better than `StringBuffer` in single-threaded scenarios due to lack of synchronization.

- **Use Case**:
  - **StringBuilder**: Preferred for use in single-threaded environments or where thread safety is managed externally.
  - **StringBuffer**: Used when thread safety is required and multiple threads are accessing the same buffer.

In general, use `StringBuilder` for better performance when synchronization is not a concern. Use `StringBuffer` only when you need thread safety.
7/22/24, 12:31 PM - 18mcmc46 Manish Choumiyan: public class StringToInt {

        int result = 0;

        for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) {
            // Get the character at position i
            char c = s.charAt(i);

            // Convert the character to its integer value and update the result

   String s = "123";
   int result = 0;
   ```

   ```java
   for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) {
       char c = s.charAt(i);
   ```
   - Loop through each character of the string `s` using its length.

   ```java
   int digit = c - '0';
   ```
   - Convert the character `c` to its integer value. This works because characters representing digits are contiguous in ASCII. For example, `'1'` is 49 and `'0'` is 48, so `'1' - '0'` equals 1.

4. **Update Result**:
   ```java
   result = result * 10 + digit;
   ```
   - Multiply the current `result` by 10 to shift its digits to the left (preparing for the next digit).
   - Add the integer value of the current character to `result`.

5. **Output the Result**:
   ```java
   System.out.println(result); // Output: 123
   ```
   - Print the final integer value after processing all characters.

**Summary**: The code manually converts a string of digits into an integer by iterating through each character, converting it to its numeric value, and accumulating the result.

- `'1'` (ASCII 49) to `1` (49 - 48)

7/22/24, 2:13 PM - 18mcmc46 Manish Choumiyan: **Comparable Interface**:
- Used for natural ordering.
- Single method: `compareTo(T o)`.
- Class implements `Comparable` to define its default sorting behavior.

Example:
```java
public class Student implements Comparable<Student> {
    private int id;
    @Override
    public int compareTo(Student other) {
        return Integer.compare(this.id, other.id);
    }
}
```

**Comparator Interface**:
- Used for custom ordering.
- Two methods: `compare(T o1, T o2)` and `equals(Object obj)`.
- Separate class or lambda expression can implement `Comparator` for different sorting criteria.

Example:
```java
public class Student {
    private int id;
    private String name;
}

Comparator<Student> byName = new Comparator<Student>() {
    @Override
    public int compare(Student s1, Student s2) {
        return s1.getName().compareTo(s2.getName());
    }
};
```
7/22/24, 2:13 PM - Quazi Mohammad Saad: Comparable and Comparator are two interfaces in Java that serve the purpose of comparing objects, but they differ in their approach and usage:

Comparable:

- Is an interface that is implemented by the class itself
- Defines a single method `compareTo()` that takes one argument
- Used to compare the current object with another object of the same class
- Returns an int value indicating the result of the comparison (negative, zero, or positive)
- Example: `String` class implements Comparable, allowing strings to be compared using `compareTo()`

Comparator:

- Is an interface that is implemented by a separate class
- Defines two methods `compare()` and `equals()`
- Used to compare two objects of the same class, but with a custom comparison logic
- Returns an int value indicating the result of the comparison (negative, zero, or positive)
- Example: `Collections.sort()` method uses a Comparator to sort a list of objects

Key differences:

- Comparable is implemented by the class itself, while Comparator is implemented by a separate class
- Comparable has a single method `compareTo()`, while Comparator has two methods `compare()` and `equals()`
- Comparable is used for natural ordering, while Comparator is used for custom ordering

When to use each:

- Use Comparable when you want to define a natural ordering for a class, and the comparison logic is simple and straightforward
- Use Comparator when you need a custom comparison logic, or when you want to compare objects of different classes

In summary, Comparable is used for natural ordering, while Comparator is used for custom ordering.

    // Development-specific data source configuration
}

7/22/24, 2:17 PM - Quazi Mohammad Saad: JDBC (Java Database Connectivity) and Hibernate are two different approaches to interact with a database in Java.

JDBC:

- Is a low-level API for connecting to a database and executing SQL queries
- Requires manual management of database connections, statements, and result sets
- Provides direct access to the database, allowing for fine-grained control
- Requires writing SQL queries manually
- Is a standard API, not a framework

Hibernate:

- Is a high-level ORM (Object-Relational Mapping) framework for mapping Java objects to database tables
- Automates database interactions, including connection management and query execution
- Provides a layer of abstraction between the application and the database
- Allows for mapping Java objects to database tables using annotations or XML configuration
- Supports caching, lazy loading, and other optimization techniques

Key differences:

- Level of abstraction: JDBC is low-level, while Hibernate is high-level
- SQL queries: JDBC requires manual SQL queries, while Hibernate generates SQL queries automatically
- Object mapping: Hibernate maps Java objects to database tables, while JDBC does not
- Connection management: Hibernate automates connection management, while JDBC requires manual management

When to use each:

- Use JDBC when:
    - You need fine-grained control over database interactions
    - You prefer writing SQL queries manually
    - You're working with a small-scale application or a simple database schema
- Use Hibernate when:
    - You want to automate database interactions and focus on business logic
    - You need to map complex Java objects to database tables
    - You're
7/22/24, 2:17 PM - 18mcmc46 Manish Choumiyan: **JDBC (Java Database Connectivity)**:
- A low-level API for interacting with relational databases.
- Requires explicit handling of SQL queries, result sets, and connections.
- Provides fine-grained control but involves more boilerplate code.

**Hibernate**:
- A high-level ORM (Object-Relational Mapping) framework.
- Automates the mapping between Java objects and database tables.
- Simplifies database operations with less boilerplate code and provides features like caching, lazy loading, and query abstraction (HQL).

Use JDBC for simple, high-performance tasks and when fine-grained control is necessary. Use Hibernate for complex applications that benefit from reduced boilerplate code and advanced ORM features.
7/22/24, 2:50 PM - 18mcmc46 Manish Choumiyan: class Solution {
    public static int findRowIndex(int[][] matrix, int target){
        int row = matrix.length;
        int col = matrix[0].length;
        int t = 0,b = row-1;
        while(t<=b){
            int m = t+(b-t)/2;
            if(matrix[m][0]<=target && target<=matrix[m][col-1]){
                return m;
            }else if(matrix[m][0]>target){
                b = m-1;
            }else{
                t = m+1;
            }
        }
        return -1;
    }
    public static boolean binarySearch(int a[],int target){
        int l = 0,r = a.length-1;
        while(l<=r){
            int m = l+(r-l)/2;
            if(a[m]==target){
                return true;
            }
            if(a[m]<target){
                l = m+1;
            }else{
                r = m-1;
            }
        }
        return false;
    }
    public boolean searchMatrix(int[][] matrix, int target) {
        int rowIndex = findRowIndex(matrix,target);
        if(rowIndex==-1){
            return false;
        }else if(matrix[rowIndex][0]==target || matrix[rowIndex][matrix[0].length-1]==target){
            return true;
        }
        int temp[] = matrix[rowIndex];
        return binarySearch(temp,target);
    }
}

7/22/24, 4:13 PM - 18mcmc11 Abhishek Pathak: Here's how you can find the third greatest element in an array in Java. This solution uses a single scan of the array to achieve \(O(n)\) complexity:

```java
public class ThirdGreatest {
    public static Integer thirdGreatest(int[] arr) {
        if (arr.length < 3) {
            return null;  // Not enough elements
        }
        
        int first = Integer.MIN_VALUE;
        int second = Integer.MIN_VALUE;
        int third = Integer.MIN_VALUE;
        
        for (int num : arr) {
            if (num > first) {
                third = second;
                second = first;
                first = num;
            } else if (num > second) {
                third = second;
                second = num;
            } else if (num > third) {
                third = num;
            }
        }
        
        return third;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int[] arr = {5, 1, 9, 6, 7, 8, 3, 2};
        Integer result = thirdGreatest(arr);
        if (result != null) {
            System.out.println("The third greatest element is " + result);
        } else {
            System.out.println("The array does not have enough elements.");
        }
    }
}
```

This program initializes three variables to hold the first, second, and third greatest values. It then iterates through the array, updating these values as needed. Finally, it prints the third greatest element, or a message if the array doesn't have enough elements.

7/22/24, 4:14 PM - 18mcmc11 Abhishek Pathak: Sure, here's a simpler solution to find the k-th greatest element by sorting the array. This method is straightforward but has a higher time complexity of \(O(n \log n)\) due to sorting.

```java
import java.util.Arrays;

public class KthGreatest {
    public static Integer kthGreatest(int[] arr, int k) {
        if (arr.length < k) {
            return null;  // Not enough elements
        }
        
        Arrays.sort(arr);
        
        return arr[arr.length - k];
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int[] arr = {5, 1, 9, 6, 7, 8, 3, 2};
        int k = 3;
        Integer result = kthGreatest(arr, k);
        if (result != null) {
            System.out.println("The " + k + "-th greatest element is " + result);
        } else {
            System.out.println("The array does not have enough elements.");
        }
    }
}
```

### Explanation:

1. **Sort the Array**:
   - Use `Arrays.sort(arr)` to sort the array in ascending order.
  
2. **Retrieve the k-th Greatest Element**:
   - After sorting, the k-th greatest element is at the index `arr.length - k`.

This solution is simple and easy to understand, making it suitable for small to moderately sized arrays.

7/22/24, 4:19 PM - 18mcmc11 Abhishek Pathak: The most efficient sorting technique depends on the specific use case and requirements. However, in general, the following are considered to be among the most efficient sorting algorithms:

1. *Quicksort*: With an average time complexity of O(n log n), Quicksort is a popular choice for many applications. It's relatively simple to implement and has a low overhead.
2. *Merge Sort*: Also with a time complexity of O(n log n), Merge Sort is a stable sort that's well-suited for large datasets and external sorting.
3. *Heap Sort*: With a time complexity of O(n log n), Heap Sort is another efficient algorithm that's simple to implement and has a low overhead.
4. *Radix Sort*: For datasets with a fixed-length integer or string keys, Radix Sort can be very efficient, with a time complexity of O(nk), where n is the number of elements and k is the number of digits in the radix sort.
5. *Timsort*: A hybrid sorting algorithm derived from merge sort and insertion sort, designed to perform well on many kinds of real-world data. It has a worst-case time complexity of O(n log n).

Consider the following factors when choosing a sorting algorithm:

- Size of the dataset
- Type of data (integer, string, etc.)
- Stability requirements (preserving order of equal elements)
- Space constraints (in-place sorting vs. extra memory)
- Implementation complexity

If you have specific requirements or constraints, I can help you choose the most efficient sorting technique for your use case!

1. _Dividing_: Split the array into two halves until each subarray has only one element.
2. _Merging_: Combine the subarrays in a sorted manner, creating a new sorted array.

The basic steps are:

1. If the array has only one element, return it (since it's already sorted).
2. Divide the array into two halves.
3. Recursively apply Merge sort to the left half.
4. Recursively apply Merge sort to the right half.
5. Merge the two sorted halves into a single sorted array.

Merge sort has several key benefits:

- _Efficient_: Merge sort has a time complexity of O(n log n), making it suitable for large datasets.
- *Stable
7/22/24, 4:23 PM - 18mcmc11 Abhishek Pathak: Selection sort is a simple sorting algorithm that works by:

1. _Selecting_ the smallest (or largest) element from the unsorted portion of the array.
2. _Swapping_ it with the first element of the unsorted portion.
3. _Repeating_ the process until the entire array is sorted.

Here's a step-by-step breakdown:

1. Start with the first element as the minimum (or maximum) element.
2. Iterate through the unsorted portion of the array to find the smallest (or largest) element.
3. Swap the found element with the first element of the unsorted portion.
4. Move to the next element and repeat steps 2-3 until the entire array is sorted.

Selection sort has a time complexity of O(n^2), making it less efficient than other algorithms like Quicksort or Merge sort for large datasets. However, it has some advantages:

- _Simple implementation_: Selection sort is easy to understand and implement.
- _Low overhead_: It requires minimal extra memory

                third = second;
                second = arr[i];
            } else if (arr[i] > third && arr[i] != second && arr[i] != first) {
                third = arr[i];
            }
        }

    }

7/22/24, 4:26 PM - Vimlendra Dubey Hcu Mca: public class BinarySearch {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int[] arr = {2, 3, 4, 10, 40};
        int target = 10;
        int result = binarySearch(arr, target);

        if (result == -1) {
            System.out.println("Element not present in the array");
        } else {
            System.out.println("Element found at index: " + result);
        }
    }

    public static int binarySearch(int[] arr, int target) {
        int left = 0;
        int right = arr.length - 1;

        while (left <= right) {
            int mid = left + (right - left) / 2;

            // Check if target is present at mid
            if (arr[mid] == target) {
                return mid;
            }

            // If target is greater, ignore the left half
            if (arr[mid] < target) {
                left = mid + 1;
            }
            // If target is smaller, ignore the right half
            else {
                right = mid - 1;
            }
        }

        // Target is not present in the array
        return -1;
    }
}

7/22/24, 4:32 PM - 18mcmc11 Abhishek Pathak: The provided code implements a binary search algorithm to find a target element in a sorted array. Binary search is an efficient algorithm with a time complexity of \(O(\log n)\). Here's a detailed explanation of the code:

### Explanation

1. **Main Method**:
   ```java
   public static void main(String[] args) {
       int[] arr = {2, 3, 4, 10, 40};
       int target = 10;
       int result = binarySearch(arr, target);

       if (result == -1) {
           System.out.println("Element not present in the array");
       } else {
           System.out.println("Element found at index: " + result);
       }
   }
   ```
   - An array `arr` is initialized with the elements `{2, 3, 4, 10, 40}`.
   - A target value `target` is set to `10`.
   - The `binarySearch` method is called with the array `arr` and the target value `10`.
   - The result of the binary search is stored in the variable `result`.
   - If `result` is `-1`, it indicates that the target element is not present in the array, and a corresponding message is printed.
   - Otherwise, the index of the found element is printed.

2. **Binary Search Method**:
   ```java
   public static int binarySearch(int[] arr, int target) {
       int left = 0;
       int right = arr.length - 1;

       while (left <= right) {
           int mid = left + (right - left) / 2;

           // Check if target is present at mid
           if (arr[mid] == target) {
               return mid;
           }

           // If target is greater, ignore the left half
           if (arr[mid] < target) {
               left = mid + 1;
           }
           // If target is smaller, ignore the right half
           else {
               right = mid - 1;
           }
       }

       // Target is not present in the array
       return -1;
   }
   ```
   - The method takes two parameters: an integer array `arr` and an integer `target`.
   - Two pointers `left` and `right` are initialized to the start and end of the array, respectively.
   - The `while` loop runs as long as `left` is less than or equal to `right`.
     - `mid` is calculated as the midpoint of the current subarray.
     - If `arr[mid]` is equal to the `target`, the index `mid` is returned.
     - If `arr[mid]` is less than the `target`, it means the target must be in the right half of the array. So, `left` is updated to `mid + 1`.
     - If `arr[mid]` is greater than the `target`, it means the target must be in the left half of the array. So, `right` is updated to `mid - 1`.
   - If the loop exits without finding the target, `-1` is returned, indicating that the target is not present in the array.

### Key Points:

- **Initialization**:
  - `left` is the index of the first element of the array.
  - `right` is the index of the last element of the array.

- **Midpoint Calculation**:
  - `mid` is calculated as `left + (right - left) / 2` to avoid potential overflow issues that can occur with `(left + right) / 2`.

- **Binary Search

7/22/24, 4:38 PM - 18mcmc11 Abhishek Pathak: To find the index of an element in a sorted array using binary search, follow these steps:

### Approach:

1. **Initialize Pointers**:
   - Set two pointers: `left` to the start of the array (index `0`) and `right` to the end of the array (index `arr.length - 1`).

2. **Iterative Search**:
   - While `left` is less than or equal to `right`:
     1. **Calculate Midpoint**:
        - Compute the midpoint index `mid` using `mid = left + (right - left) / 2`.
     2. **Compare Target with Midpoint**:
        - If `arr[mid]` is equal to the target, return `mid`.
        - If the target is greater than `arr[mid]`, update `left` to `mid + 1`.
        - If the target is smaller than `arr[mid]`, update `right` to `mid - 1`.

3. **Handle Element Not Found**:
   - If the loop exits without finding the target, return `-1`.

### Summary:

1. **Initialization**: Set `left` to `0` and `right` to `arr.length - 1`.
2. **Loop**:
   - Compute `mid`.
   - Compare `arr[mid]` with the target:
     - If equal, return `mid`.
     - If target is greater, adjust `left`.
     - If target is smaller, adjust `right`.
3. **End**:
   - Return `-1` if the target is not found.

7/22/24, 4:40 PM - Vimlendra Dubey Hcu Mca: public class RotatedBinarySearch {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int[] arr = {7, 8, 9, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5};
        int target = 3;
        int result = search(arr, target);

        if (result == -1) {
            System.out.println("Element not present in the array");
        } else {
            System.out.println("Element found at index: " + result);
        }
    }

    public static int search(int[] arr, int target) {
        int left = 0;
        int right = arr.length - 1;

        while (left <= right) {
            int mid = left + (right - left) / 2;

            // Check if target is present at mid
            if (arr[mid] == target) {
                return mid;
            }

            // Determine which side is properly sorted
            if (arr[left] <= arr[mid]) {
                // Left half is sorted
                if (arr[left] <= target && target < arr[mid]) {
                    right = mid - 1;
                } else {
                    left = mid + 1;
                }
            } else {
                // Right half is sorted
                if (arr[mid] < target && target <= arr[right]) {
                    left = mid + 1;
                } else {
                    right = mid - 1;
                }
            }
        }

        // Target is not present in the array
        return -1;
    }
}

```
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int a = 10;
        int b = 20;

        System.out.println("Before swapping: a = " + a + ", b = " + b);

        // Swap 'a' and 'b'
        a = a + b; // a now becomes 30
        b = a - b; // b becomes 10
        a = a - b; // a becomes 20

        System.out.println("After swapping: a = " + a + ", b = " + b);
    }
}
```

However, please note that this approach can lead to overflow errors if the sum of the two numbers exceeds the maximum limit of the data type.

```
List<Employee> employees = ...; // your employee list

employees.stream()
    .filter(e -> e.getAge() > 45)
    .forEach(e -> System.out.println(e.getName() + ": " + (e.getAge() * e.getSalary())));

*Flow:*

1. *Controller*:
    - Receive HTTP request (e.g., GET, POST, PUT, DELETE)
    - Map request to handler method using `@RequestMapping`
    - Extract request parameters, headers, and body (if applicable)
    - Call Service layer method, passing required data
2. *Service*:
    - Receive data from Controller
    - Perform business logic operations (e.g., calculations, validations)
    - Call Repository layer method, passing required data
    - Receive data from Repository layer
    - Return processed data to Controller
3. *Repository*:
    - Receive data from Service layer
    - Interact with database (e.g., CRUD operations) using JPA or JDBC
    - Return data to Service layer
4. *Controller (continued)*:
    - Receive processed data from Service layer
    - Return response to client (e.g., JSON, HTML)

*Example:*

- Controller: `UserController`
- Service: `UserService`
- Repository: `UserRepository`

*Flow Example:*

1. `UserController` receives a GET request to retrieve a user by ID.
2. `UserController` calls `UserService` method `getUserById(Long id)`.

7/24/24, 11:56 AM - 18mcmc11 Abhishek Pathak: In Spring Boot, configuration properties can be specified in either `application.properties` or `application.yml` (YAML format). Both files serve the same purpose of externalizing configuration, but they differ in syntax and structure. Here are the key differences:

### `application.properties`
- **Syntax**: Uses key-value pairs, where each property is defined on a separate line.
- **Flat Structure**: Properties are flat, and hierarchical relationships are represented using dots in property keys.

#### Example:
```properties
server.port=8080
spring.datasource.url=jdbc:mysql://localhost:3306/mydb
spring.datasource.username=root
spring.datasource.password=password

# Nested properties for complex structures
spring.datasource.hikari.maximum-pool-size=10
```

### `application.yml`
- **Syntax**: Uses YAML syntax, which is more readable for hierarchical data. Indentation is significant and represents nested structures.
- **Hierarchical Structure**: Properties are naturally nested, which makes it easier to represent complex configurations.

It was introduced in Java 8 to allow interfaces to provide a basic implementation for methods, which can be overridden by implementing classes if needed.

Default methods are declared using the `default` keyword and can contain code, unlike abstract methods. They are useful for:

1. Adding new methods to an interface without breaking existing implementations.
2. Providing a basic implementation that can be shared by multiple classes.
3. Reducing code duplication.

```
public interface Printable {
    default void print() {
        System.out.println("

7/24/24, 11:58 AM - 18mcmc11 Abhishek Pathak: Runnable
7/24/24, 11:59 AM - 18mcmc27 Abhinav Gautam: Here's an example of using the Runnable interface in Java:

```
// Implementing Runnable interface
class MyRunnable implements Runnable {
    @Override
    public void run() {
        System.out.println("Hello from MyRunnable!");
    }
}

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Creating an instance of MyRunnable
        MyRunnable myRunnable = new MyRunnable();
        
        // Creating a Thread and passing myRunnable to it
        Thread thread = new Thread(myRunnable);
        
        // Starting the Thread
        thread.start();
    }
}
```

In this example:

1. We create a class `MyRunnable` that implements the `Runnable` interface.
2. We override the `run()` method, which contains the code we want to execute in a separate thread.
3. In the `Main` class, we create an instance of `MyRunnable` and pass it to a new `Thread` object.
4. We start the thread using the `start()` method, which executes the `run()` method in a separate thread.

When you run this program, it will print "Hello from MyRunnable!" to the console, demonstrating that the code in the `run()` method is executed in a separate thread.

Alternatively, you can also use a lambda expression to implement the Runnable interface:

```
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Creating a Thread with a lambda expression
        Thread thread = new Thread(() -> System.out.println("Hello from lambda!"));
        
        // Starting the Thread
        thread.start();
    }
}
```

This achieves the same result as the previous example but with a more concise syntax.

7/24/24, 12:00 PM - 18mcmc11 Abhishek Pathak: The `Runnable` interface in Java is part of the `java.lang` package and is designed to be implemented by any class whose instances are intended to be executed by a thread. The `Runnable` interface should be implemented by classes that want to define a task that can be run concurrently with other tasks.

### Key Points
- The `Runnable` interface has a single method: `run()`.
- The `run()` method contains the code that constitutes the new thread's task.
- Instances of `Runnable` can be executed by a thread, typically by passing the `Runnable` instance to a `Thread` object and then starting the thread.

### Runnable Interface Definition
```java
public interface Runnable {
    void run();
}
```

### Example Code Using Runnable Interface
Here’s an example that demonstrates how to implement and use the `Runnable` interface:

#### Example 1: Basic Implementation
1. **Define a class that implements `Runnable`**:
   ```java
   public class MyRunnable implements Runnable {
       @Override
       public void run() {
           for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
               System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " - Count: " + i);
               try {
                   Thread.sleep(1000); // Sleep for 1 second
               } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                   e.printStackTrace();
               }
           }
       }
   }
   ```

2. **Create and start threads using the `MyRunnable` class**:
   ```java
   public class RunnableExample {
       public static void main(String[] args) {
           MyRunnable myRunnable = new MyRunnable();

           Thread thread1 = new Thread(myRunnable, "Thread-1");
           Thread thread2 = new Thread(myRunnable, "Thread-2");

           thread1.start();
           thread2.start();
       }
   }
   ```

### Explanation
1. **MyRunnable Class**:
   - Implements the `Runnable` interface.
   - The `run` method contains the code to be executed by the thread. It prints the thread name and a counter, then sleeps for 1 second.

2. **RunnableExample Class**:
   - Creates instances of `Thread` and passes the `MyRunnable` instance to their constructors.
   - Starts the threads using the `start` method, which in turn calls the `run` method of `MyRunnable`.

### Example 2: Using Lambda Expression (Java 8 and Later)
Java 8 introduced lambda expressions, making it easier to create instances of `Runnable`. Here's how you can use a lambda expression with `Runnable`:

```java
public class RunnableLambdaExample {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Runnable myRunnable = () -> {
            for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
                System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " - Count: " + i);
                try {
                    Thread.sleep(1000); // Sleep for 1 second
                } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                    e.printStackTrace();
                }
            }
        };

        Thread thread1 = new Thread(myRunnable, "Thread-1");
        Thread thread2 = new Thread(myRunnable, "Thread-2");

        thread1.start();
        thread2.start();
    }
}
```

### Explanation
- **Lambda Expression**: Provides a more concise way to create a `Runnable` instance. The code within the lambda expression is the implementation of the `run` method.
- **Thread Creation and Start**: Similar to the previous example,

7/24/24, 12:06 PM - 18mcmc27 Abhinav Gautam: Here's a SQL query to find the student with the maximum marks in each section (A, B, C):

```
SELECT 
  Section, 
  Name, 
  MAX(Marks) as Max_Marks
FROM 
  Student
GROUP BY 
  Section
```

This query will return the student with the maximum marks in each section. However, if there are multiple students with the same maximum marks in a section, this query will only return one of them.

To return all students with maximum marks in each section, you can use the following query:

```
SELECT 
  s1.Section, 
  s1.Name, 
  s1.Marks
FROM 
  Student s1
WHERE 
  s1.Marks = (
    SELECT 
      MAX(s2.Marks)
    FROM 
      Student s2
    WHERE 
      s2.Section = s1.Section
  )
```

This query will return all students who have the maximum marks in their respective sections.

Note: Replace "Student" with your actual table name, and "Section", "Name", and "Marks" with your actual column names.

7/25/24, 8:40 PM - 18mcmc11 Abhishek Pathak: The four pillars of Object-Oriented Programming (OOP) are:

1. *Encapsulation*: This concept binds together the data and the methods that manipulate that data, keeping both safe from outside interference and misuse. In other words, it hides the implementation details of an object from the outside world.

2. *Abstraction*: This concept shows only the necessary information to the outside world while hiding the internal details. It helps to reduce complexity and increase focus on essential features.

3. *Inheritance*: This concept allows one class to inherit the properties and behavior of another class. The child class inherits all the fields and methods of the parent class and can also add new fields and methods or override the ones inherited from the parent class.

4. *Polymorphism*: This concept allows objects of different classes to be treated as objects of a common superclass. It provides a way to perform a single action in different ways, depending on the object's class. This can be achieved through method overriding or method overloading.

These four pillars work together to make OOP a powerful and flexible programming paradigm. They help developers create reusable, maintainable, and scalable code. Do you have any specific questions about these pillars? I'm here to help!
7/25/24, 8:42 PM - 18mcmc27 Abhinav Gautam: OOPS concepts can be applied in automation testing in several ways:

1. _Encapsulation_: Hide implementation details of test scripts and expose only necessary information through public methods.

2. _Abstraction_: Create abstract classes or interfaces for test scripts, allowing for polymorphic behavior.

3. _Inheritance_: Create a base test class with common methods and inherit it in specific test classes.

4. _Polymorphism_: Use method overriding or method overloading to execute different test scenarios.

5. _Composition_: Combine multiple test scripts or classes to create a larger test suite.

Example:

```
// Abstract class for test scripts
public abstract class TestScript {
    public abstract void setup();
    public abstract void execute();
    public abstract void tearDown();
}

// Specific test class inheriting from TestScript
public class LoginTest extends TestScript {
    @Override
    public void setup() {
        // Setup code
    }

    @Override
    public void execute() {
        // Execution code
    }

    @Override
    public void tearDown() {
        // Teardown code
    }
}

// Test suite class using composition
public class TestSuite {
    private List<TestScript> tests = new ArrayList<>();

    public void addTest(TestScript test) {
        tests.add(test);
    }

    public void executeTests() {
        for (TestScript test : tests) {
            test.setup();
            test.execute();
            test.tearDown();
        }
    }
}
```

In this example, we use OOPS concepts to create a modular and extensible test framework. The `TestScript` abstract class provides a contract for test scripts, while the `LoginTest` class implements specific test logic. The `TestSuite` class uses composition to combine multiple test scripts and execute them.
7/25/24, 9:04 PM - Quazi Mohammad Saad: ...import java.util.PriorityQueue;

public class KthLargestElement {
    public static int findKthLargest(int[] nums, int k) {
        PriorityQueue<Integer> minHeap = new PriorityQueue<>();

        for (int num : nums) {
            minHeap.add(num);
            if (minHeap.size() > k) {
                minHeap.poll(); // Remove the smallest element in the heap
            }
        }

        return minHeap.peek(); // The root of the heap is the k-th largest element
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int[] nums = {3, 2, 1, 5, 6, 4};
        int k = 2;
        System.out.println("The " + k + "th largest element is " + findKthLargest(nums, k));
    }
}
7/25/24, 9:04 PM - Quazi Mohammad Saad: ExplanationPriority Queue (Min-Heap): We use a PriorityQueue to act as a min-heap.Add Elements: Iterate through the array, adding each element to the min-heap.Maintain Size of Heap: Ensure the heap size does not exceed k. If it does, remove the smallest element using poll().Retrieve k-th Largest Element: Once the iteration is complete, the root of the min-heap (peek()) will be the k-th largest element.
7/25/24, 9:05 PM - Quazi Mohammad Saad: Explanation

public class KthLargestElement {
    public static int findKthLargest(int[] nums, int k) {
        Arrays.sort(nums); // Sort the array in ascending order
        return nums[nums.length - k]; // The k-th largest element is at index length - k
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int[] nums = {3, 2, 1, 5, 6, 4};
        int k = 2;
        System.out.println("The " + k + "th largest element is " + findKthLargest(nums, k));
    }
}

7/25/24, 9:10 PM - 18mcmc46 Manish Choumiyan: To write a new microservice that communicates with other microservices, follow these steps:

1. **Define API Contracts:**
   - Ensure all microservices have well-defined APIs, usually documented with OpenAPI/Swagger.

2. **Choose Communication Protocol:**
   - Use HTTP/REST or gRPC for synchronous communication.
   - Use messaging systems (e.g., RabbitMQ, Kafka) for asynchronous communication.

3. **Implement API Client:**
   - Use libraries like `RestTemplate` or `WebClient` for HTTP calls in Spring Boot.
   - For gRPC, use the gRPC client library.

4. **Error Handling and Retries:**
   - Implement error handling and retry logic for network failures or service unavailability.

5. **Service Discovery:**
   - Use a service registry (e.g., Eureka) to dynamically discover and communicate with other services.

6. **Security:**
   - Implement security (e.g., OAuth2, JWT) to secure communication between microservices.

7. **Configuration Management:**
   - Use a centralized configuration service (e.g., Spring Cloud Config) for managing settings.

8. **Logging and Monitoring:**
   - Implement distributed logging (e.g., with ELK Stack) and monitoring (e.g., Prometheus, Grafana) for tracking interactions between services.

### Example (Using Spring Boot and REST):

```java
import org.springframework.beans.factory.annotation.Autowired;
import org.springframework.web.bind.annotation.GetMapping;
import org.springframework.web.bind.annotation.RequestMapping;
import org.springframework.web.bind.annotation.RestController;
import org.springframework.web.client.RestTemplate;

@RestController
@RequestMapping("/api")
public class MyMicroserviceController {

    @Autowired
    private RestTemplate restTemplate;

    @GetMapping("/data")
    public String getDataFromOtherService() {
        String otherServiceUrl = "http://other-service/api/data";
        return restTemplate.getForObject(otherServiceUrl, String.class);
    }
}
```

### Application Configuration:

```yaml
# application.yml
spring:
  application:
    name: my-microservice
  cloud:
    discovery:
      client:
        service-url:
          default: http://localhost:8761/eureka/
```

This setup assumes you are using Eureka for service discovery and `RestTemplate` for making HTTP requests. Adjust based on your specific architecture and tools.

1. **Load Balancing:**
   - Distribute incoming requests across multiple servers using load balancers (e.g., NGINX, HAProxy, AWS ELB).

2. **Caching:**
   - Use caching mechanisms to reduce the load on backend systems (e.g., Redis, Memcached).

3. **Rate Limiting:**
   - Implement rate limiting to control the number of requests a client can make in a given time period.

4. **Scaling:**
   - **Horizontal Scaling:** Add more instances of your service to handle increased load.
   - **Vertical Scaling:** Increase resources (CPU, memory) for existing instances.

5. **Asynchronous Processing:**
   - Offload long-running tasks to background workers or message queues.

6. **Database Optimization:**
   - Optimize database queries, use indexing, and implement database connection pooling.

7. **API Throttling:**
   - Implement throttling to manage and control traffic, preventing any one service from being overwhelmed.

8. **Monitoring and Alerts:**
   - Monitor API performance and set up alerts for unusual activity or high load using tools like Prometheus, Grafana, or CloudWatch.

9. **Circuit Breaker:**
   - Use circuit breakers (e.g., Hystrix) to prevent failures from cascading and to handle fault tolerance.

10. **Content Delivery Network (CDN):**
    - Use CDNs to offload static content delivery and reduce server load.

Applying these strategies can help maintain API performance and reliability under varying loads.

1. _Monitoring tools_: Use monitoring tools like Prometheus, Grafana, New Relic, or Datadog to track service performance metrics.

2. _Logging_: Analyze logs to identify slow requests, errors, or bottlenecks.

7/25/24, 9:22 PM - 18mcmc46 Manish Choumiyan: To make a piece of code thread-safe, follow these guidelines:

1. **Synchronization:**
   - Use synchronized blocks or methods to ensure that only one thread can execute a critical section of code at a time.
   ```java
   synchronized (lock) {
       // critical section
   }
   ```

2. **Concurrent Collections:**
   - Use thread-safe collections from `java.util.concurrent` package (e.g., `ConcurrentHashMap`, `CopyOnWriteArrayList`).

3. **Atomic Variables:**
   - Use atomic classes (e.g., `AtomicInteger`, `AtomicLong`) for thread-safe operations on single variables.
   ```java
   AtomicInteger count = new AtomicInteger(0);
   count.incrementAndGet();
   ```

4. **Immutability:**
   - Design immutable objects where possible. Once created, their state cannot be changed, which avoids synchronization issues.

5. **Thread-Local Variables:**
   - Use `ThreadLocal` to maintain data that is local to a thread and not shared among threads.
   ```java
   ThreadLocal<Integer> threadLocal = ThreadLocal.withInitial(() -> 0);
   ```

6. **Locks:**
   - Use explicit locking mechanisms from `java.util.concurrent.locks` package (e.g., `ReentrantLock`).
   ```java
   ReentrantLock lock = new ReentrantLock();
   lock.lock();
   try {
       // critical section
   } finally {
       lock.unlock();
   }
   ```

7. **Volatile Keyword:**
   - Use the `volatile` keyword for variables that are accessed by multiple threads to ensure visibility of changes across threads.
   ```java
   private volatile boolean flag = false;
   ```

8. **Use of Executors:**
   - Use `ExecutorService` to manage threads and tasks, which handles synchronization internally.
   ```java
   ExecutorService executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(10);
   executor.submit(() -> {
       // task
   });
   ```

Applying these techniques helps ensure that your code behaves correctly and efficiently in a multi-threaded environment.

7/25/24, 9:23 PM - 18mcmc46 Manish Choumiyan: Here’s a comparison between `Atomic` and `synchronized` in Java:

### Atomic

- **Purpose:** Provides atomic operations on single variables.
- **Implementation:** Uses low-level atomic CPU instructions for thread-safe operations.
- **Usage:** Ideal for simple variables and operations like incrementing or comparing values.
- **Performance:** Generally faster and more efficient for single-variable operations due to reduced overhead.
- **Classes:** `AtomicInteger`, `AtomicLong`, `AtomicReference`, etc.

**Example:**
```java
AtomicInteger count = new AtomicInteger(0);
count.incrementAndGet(); // thread-safe increment
```

### Synchronized

- **Purpose:** Provides mutual exclusion to ensure that only one thread can execute a block of code at a time.
- **Implementation:** Uses intrinsic locks to protect critical sections of code.
- **Usage:** Suitable for more complex operations or multiple variables where atomic operations are insufficient.
- **Performance:** Can be slower due to lock acquisition and release overhead, especially in highly contended situations.
- **Usage Pattern:** Applied to methods or code blocks using the `synchronized` keyword.

**Example:**
```java
private int count = 0;

public synchronized void increment() {
    count++;
}
```

### Summary

- **Atomic:** Best for simple, high-performance scenarios involving individual variables.
- **Synchronized:** Suitable for complex scenarios requiring protection of critical sections or multiple shared variables.

7/25/24, 9:25 PM - 18mcmc11 Abhishek Pathak: JSON Web Tokens (JWT) is a compact, URL-safe means of representing claims to be transferred between two parties. It's a digitally signed token that contains a payload (e.g., user data) and is used for authentication and authorization.

Here's how JWT works:

1. *Token creation*: A server generates a JWT token by:
    - Creating a payload with user data (e.g., username, email)
    - Signing the payload with a secret key using an algorithm (e.g., HMAC SHA256)
    - Encoding the payload and signature into a URL-safe string
2. *Token transmission*: The server sends the JWT token to the client (e.g., web browser or mobile app)
3. *Token verification*: The client sends the JWT token back to the server with each request
    - The server verifies the token by:
        - Decoding the token
        - Checking the signature using the same secret key and algorithm
        - Validating the payload (e.g., checking expiration time)

If the token is valid, the server grants access to protected resources.

*JWT structure*:

- *Header*: Contains the algorithm and token type (e.g., `{"alg": "HS256", "typ": "JWT"}`)
- *Payload*: Contains the user data (e.g., `
7/25/24, 9:25 PM - 18mcmc11 Abhishek Pathak: JSON Web Tokens (JWT) is a compact, URL-safe means of representing claims to be transferred between two parties. It's a digitally signed token that contains a payload (e.g., user data) and is used for authentication and authorization.

Here's how JWT works:

1. *Token creation*: A server generates a JWT token by:
    - Creating a payload with user data (e.g., username, email)
    - Signing the payload with a secret key using an algorithm (e.g., HMAC SHA256)
    - Encoding the payload and signature into a URL-safe string
2. *Token transmission*: The server sends the JWT token to the client (e.g., web browser or mobile app)
3. *Token verification*: The client sends the JWT token back to the server with each request
    - The server verifies the token by:
        - Decoding the token
        - Checking the signature using the same secret key and algorithm
        - Validating the payload (e.g., checking expiration time)

If the token is valid, the server grants access to protected resources.

*JWT structure*:

- *Header*: Contains the algorithm and token type (e.g., `{"alg": "HS256", "typ": "JWT"}`)
- *Payload*: Contains the user data (e.g., `{"username": "john", "email": "john@example.com"}`)
- *Signature*: Contains the digitally signed payload (e.g., `HMACSHA256(base64UrlEncode(header) + "." + base64UrlEncode(payload), secretKey)`)

*Advantages*:

- Statelessness: No need to store session information on the server
- Scalability: Easy to distribute and verify tokens across multiple servers
- Security: Digitally signed tokens prevent tampering and ensure authenticity

*Common use cases*:

- Authentication and authorization
- Single sign-on (SSO) solutions
- API security and access control
- Microservices communication

Remember to keep your secret key secure and use HTTPS to transmit JWT tokens to prevent interception and tampering.
7/25/24, 9:27 PM - Quazi Mohammad Saad: The time duration that a JWT (JSON Web Token) token is active is determined by the server that issues the token. This duration is known as the token's "lifetime" or "expiration time".

When a server returns a JWT token to a client, the token typically contains an "exp" (expiration) claim, which specifies the timestamp at which the token expires. This timestamp is usually represented as a Unix timestamp (seconds since January 1, 1970, 00:00:00 UTC).

The expiration time is usually set by the server when generating the token, and it can vary depending on the use case and security requirements. Common expiration times for JWT tokens include:

- Short-lived tokens: 15 minutes to 1 hour (e.g., for authentication purposes)
- Medium-lived tokens: 1 hour to 24 hours (e.g., for API access)
- Long-lived tokens: 1 day to 1 week (e.g., for remember-me functionality)

After the token expires, the client must request a new token from the server to maintain access to protected resources.

It's important to note that the server can also revoke tokens before their expiration time, if needed, by maintaining a token blacklist or using token revocation mechanisms.

In summary, the time duration that a JWT token is active depends on the expiration time set by the server, which can vary based on the specific use case and security requirements.
7/25/24, 9:27 PM - Quazi Mohammad Saad: To validate a JSON Web Token (JWT), follow these steps:

1. *Check the token format*: Ensure the token is in the correct format, consisting of three parts separated by dots (header.payload.signature).

2. *Verify the header*: Check the header for the correct algorithm (e.g., HS256, RS256).

3. *Verify the signature*:
    - Use the server's secret key (for symmetric algorithms like HS256) or public key (for asymmetric algorithms like RS256).
    - Recalculate the signature using the header and payload.
    - Compare the recalculated signature with the provided signature.

4. *Verify the payload*:
    - Check the issuer (iss) matches the expected issuer.
    - Check the audience (aud) matches the expected audience.
    - Check the expiration time (exp) has not passed.
    - Check the issued-at time (iat) is reasonable.
    - Check any other custom claims as needed.

5. *Check for token blacklisting*: Verify the token has not been revoked or blacklisted.

You can use libraries like:

- *jsonwebtoken* (Node.js)
- *jjwt* (Java)
- *PyJWT* (Python)
- *System.IdentityModel.Tokens.Jwt* (.NET)
